

=== What You'll Learn: AI Testing, LLM Evaluation & the Tools That Power It ===


--- Why Testing LLM Apps Is Completely Different from Traditional Testing ---

Welcome to the first section of our course testing large language model applications using table ragas
and more tools using Olama and local large language model.
And in this section we are going to talk about testing and also known as evaluating our large language
models.
So we'll discuss how we can evaluate our large language models.
Well why should we really evaluate and large language model in first place.
We now to discuss this we need to first understand this part.
As you can see over here, we as a user will send a prompt to the large language model.
It can be either a local large language model or it can be a large language model which is hosted on
the cloud, like ChatGPT for that matter, or deep Sikh for that matter.
So if you're going to be asking a question to Deep Sikh over here, we are going to get a response back
from the large language model.
I mean, if a user give a question or a prompt, then we get a response back, which is great.
But now we need to evaluate the response that we get from the large language model is correct or not.
I mean, if you know the answer already, we know that this response that we are getting from the large
language model is correct or wrong.
But if we do not know the answer, then it could be tricky.
For example, as you can see over here, I'm going to ask a question here saying what is the capital
of New Zealand?
Just give the city name.
So I'm just restricting the large language model to just give me a city name.
So if I ask this question right now, the large language model is going to give me a response saying
Wellington.
So I'm going to open ChatGPT window over here and I'm going to say, what is the capital of NZ?
Just give me the city name and you will see that immediately.
The ChatGPT is going to give me the response saying Wellington, which is great.
So we are going to get this particular answer as discussed in the diagram over here.
Amazing.
But now we are going to go a level further.
So now over here I have given a entire document to our large language model.
And then I'm going to ask the question to the large language model that write a summary of my document.
And now this large language model is going to read the document, and then it's going to give you a
summary of the document that it has read the same thing.
You can actually do it from here as well.
So you can upload a file, and then you can start asking the question based on that particular file
from a ChatGPT or even Gemini for that matter.
So if you try to do that, the response is going to come out and you can verify the response is going
to come from the large language model is correct or not based on your understanding of the document
already.
But if you think that the response is going to be coming out from the large language model is always
correct, you may be in trouble because we can't 100% predict that the response is going to come out
from this large language model can be accurate, and that's the reason why we need to do some testing
out from there.
And that's when the testing starts kicking in.
So you see here this is a very straightforward question.
And you are going to get the response back.
So that is pretty obvious.
So I mean, you can do a quick test based on the response that you get.
But over here while you give a document and then you get a response like a summary of the document,
you may probably need to verify that the summary is correct or not.
So that's when the QA hat comes in.
And then what if you ask a question saying write a C sharp dotnet code for the playwright test.
Now the deep seek is going to give you a response.
It's a code block.
And now you may be thinking that the code block is given back from the deep C.
R1 could be correct, but if you try to run this code in your IDE, that code might not work.
And if you just, uh, I mean, if you then go and ask the large language model, can you try to fix
this issue, which I'm currently getting based on the code that you have generated, then the deep C
will go and say, ah, sorry, I just made a mistake and I'm going to go and fix this code for you and
then it gives you a new code.
I mean, we can't really always 100% Be sure that the code which is generated by this large language
model can be 100% accurate.
And that's the reason why we have to ensure that the response that we get back from the large language
model is always correct or not.
And that's when the evaluation kicks in.
So hope you got the idea why we should do the evaluation of a large language model based applications.
And now let's get our attention back to what is large language models evaluation.
Well, Llms are typically evaluated using a standardized set of data set designed to assess their performance
across a diverse range of tasks, such as text summarization.
That we just saw an example are a open book question answering.
So you can ask a question like what is the capital of New Zealand?
Then it's going to give you the answer saying, oh, there we go, it's Wellington.
So that is an open book question answering that you can ask as well.
And similarly you can ask for the code generation like write a C sharp code.
And then it will give you an answer based on that and the language understanding, you can verify whether
the language that you're going to give in is going to be understood by the large language model, and
then it's going to give you the response back.
And these data sets serves as a benchmark to provide a fair and comparative analysis of different models,
helping researchers and developers gauge their effectiveness in handling real world scenarios.
This is quite important because while we go in an organization where we are going to be testing these
large language model, we need to understand or assess if this response that we're going to get from
the LM is always quite right or not.
And then evaluation metrics.
And this is something very important.
This is where you're going to be having all the metrics.
You're going to hold all the metrics to ensure that the response that you're going to be testing against
the large language model is correct or not.
So LM evaluation metrics will fall in two broad categories.
One is the traditional and another one is the non traditional metrics.
So traditional metrics focuses on word order and structures comparing generated text against a reference,
or that is called as the ground truth, and the example includes exact match or bleu score.
Rogue and F1 scores commonly used for tasks like summarization and translation.
So these are some of the, uh, some of the some of the way or techniques that you can actually use
to verify your large language models response against the ground truth, which is going to act as a
reference for your evaluation.
We are going to be using this reference quite a lot in our testing, because we are going to verify
that this is the response that I'm going to be getting back from or expecting from our large language
model.
And this is the response that I'm going to get back from the large language model application.
Now I need to compare the actual versus expected.
So that's when the reference or the otherwise called the ground truth is very, very important.
And we are going to verify based on the exact match Bleu score, rogue and F1 score.
To see whether the application really behaves as expected or the large language models application is
really giving us the response that we are looking for.
And there is also this non-traditional metrics leverages a semantic understanding and the model's own
capability to assess the generated text, and these can work with or without a reference text using
a techniques like embedding similarities, perplexity, LM based scoring, and more nuanced evaluation.
And this is something that you are going to be doing while you actually fine tune a large language model
and verify whether the large language model is working as expected.
And also what is the scoring or evaluation scoring that you get from the large language models based
on the fine tuning that you are trying to do.
So those evaluations, we can do it as well, and then we can verify how things are working.
And that's when these hugging face evaluate method comes in very handy to verify how you can evaluate
a large language models output based on the given input that you're going to be getting.
So I mean, those are things that we can use to evaluate the large language models application as well
as the model itself.
And there are many other evaluation techniques available.
We are going to talk about these evaluation metrics later in our upcoming lectures of this section.
But you may notice that over here there is this intellisense from Visual Studio Code that I have taken
is actually from the deep eval.
And you can see that there are so many different metrics available.
So answer relevance and then base conversational metrics, base metrics, base multi-modal metrics.
You see that how many different metrics are available.
There are many different metrics.
And the scroll bar is over here.

--- LLM Evaluation 101: The Different Ways to Measure AI Quality ---

In this video, we're going to talk about the basics of large language model evaluation with prompts.
So we know that the large language model and the field of artificial intelligence have become powerful
tools for the business across various industries.
But in order to unlock the full potential of these models, we require not only the careful implementation,
but also careful evaluation and optimization.
And that is where this prompt evaluations are often called as evolves have become very, very crucial.
So it's about like how you really evaluate your large language model to understand how you can implement
it in a more better fashion.
Well, this is the fact that I have taken from the Cloud Solution architect, who told that the inability
for the team to measure the performance of their model is the biggest blocker of production use case
for llms, and also makes prompting an art instead of science.
The prompting is like an art instead of a sign, so prompting takes first importance precedence.
While we try to do the evaluation of our large language model and an AI application.
And even though eval takes a lot of time, doing them upfront will save developers time in the long
run and results in a better product getting out much sooner.
That is quite true.
That's the reason why prompting and eval just goes hand in hand, because these are quite an intrinsic
part.
So while you try to do an evaluation of your large language model, prompting plays a key role in order
to make that happen.
And that's the reason why I wanted to show you this fact that we have to do.
And in this entire course, we are going to be talking about evaluation a lot like using the tools of
deep or raagas or hugging face valve.
The first truth is that we need to, first of all, understand how we can write a better prompt in order
to make the evaluation more smoother.
But you may have a question asking why is this really happening?
Why is the prompting is so hard, and why developers are not really doing the prompt well.
The fact is, many peoples are unfamiliar with the entire idea of the evaluations, and also it's unclear
of how to implement these evaluation.
And that's the reason why prompting is not really given.
More presence and evaluation becomes harder and harder, and sometimes it's even been left out, like
how the unit testing has been left out in most of the companies.
So evaluation of large language model, as you can see right now, is really, really important as shown
like the how the solution architect of cloud anthropic team has told.
And also there are even more benefits of these evaluation.
Evaluation will do iterative prompting improvement and also quality assurance before and after the deployment
model comparison for objective and also helps us to do the cost saving.
So as you can see over here, every single time when we try to do the model evaluation, the iterative
prompt improvement will make the evaluation even more better.
I will show you in our next video how we can do that using the human based evaluation.
But every single time when you try to do more and more evaluation, you are going to see the iterative
improvement of your prompting.
And at the same time, you will start evaluating your large language model in a better fashion.
And this will in turn do a helpful comparison of your model.
And also it does a lot of different cost saving for your company.
But the next question is how do we do this evaluation?
Well, the evaluation process starts something like this.
We are going to do a test case writing.
And then we will write multiple different prompts in this test cases and also the expected outputs and
things of that nature.
And then we will be asked as a prompt to the large language model.
We'll run some evaluation tools.
And that's the reason why this course exists.
We are going to use many different tools like deep or ragas or hugging face evaluate and even more to
do a better evaluation.
And finally, once the expected results is achieved, and once we are happy with these results, we
then deploy these models to the production.
That is how this entire process really happens.
But because this kind of evaluation process is not like a classical testing, because as you see the
test case over here, you may immediately think that how is this test case is going to look like, well
guess what, you're quite right.
This test cases are not as classical as how we used to do the testing.
And that is the reason why, in order to do the evaluation of our large language model, the test case
will have these inputs.
It is going to have an input data, a golden data which is the expected results, and then the model
output tool outputs and tool selections and also a score.
So you can see that this input data is nothing but the data that we are asking in to the large language
model, like how we ask ChatGPT before in our earlier lecture to ask a question.
And then we got the response that's the input data.
And the response that we are getting can be of any data from the large language model, but we can keep
our golden data like what is the expected result that we are looking for?
While we use the tools like ragas and deep.
We are going to be using this golden data set to create the data for our large language model evaluation
as well.
So keep remembering this golden data.
We are going to refer the same terminology in our upcoming sections of this course.
And then we're going to have a model outputs.
And this is the actual output from your large language model.
It could be from your AI applications.
It could be from a Rag application.
This is the actual output that is going to be coming in.
And if you're going to be using any of the tools like AI agents, then it's going to also have a tool
outputs.
And also we will be evaluating how we do verify the right tool is selected for a given job.
So we can also do the evaluation of that.
And finally we can also check the score of these evaluation results.
So this is how the evaluation really happens behind the scene and this is what you do.
Input as an test case for your actual large language model to do the evaluation.
Well, as that said, the final thing is what are the different types of evaluation really exist?
Well, there are three types of evaluation exist.
One is the human based evaluation or otherwise called as the human graded evaluation, and the code
graded evaluation or code based evaluation and large language model based evaluation or large language
model as a judge.
So these are the three different types of evaluation does exist.
And if you remember and while reading this human based evaluation, you may think like can a human do
the evaluation?
Well, yes, that's exactly what we have done.
In even an earlier lecture, while we were trying to enter some input in the ChatGPT, we got the response
back.
So when it comes to the task that needs a bit of a human touch or a subjective opinions, the human
grading is still the best way to go with.
And this means having people or usually expert in this field Look at the model's results.
Check if they are good and give some score.
That is what is called as the human based evaluation.
For example, if you are a doctor and if you are prescribing some medicine for a given input, you know
things better.
But if you are gonna do the same kind of testing with a software test engineer, he may not be clear
about the actual output that is going to be returned back from the large language model.
Maybe the medicines chemical combination is quite similar to what is being expected, but the test engineer
may put it fail because that is not what the expected output is.
But a person who is good in this field can easily tell that this is the actual output, which can also
be a combination, and it is the correct result.
That is how the human evaluation plays a picture, and it's usually the expert in the field who does
this job.
And then code based evaluation.
Well, code based grading or code based evaluation implies a programmatic method to assess the model's
output.
And this approach is particularly suitable for a task that is, uh, that have an unambiguous Ambiguous
and objective criteria, and we are going to mostly use this code based approach.
While we do the hugging face evaluate.
But guess what?
The next approach that we are going to talk about is the LMS based evaluation.
So this LMS based grading strikes the balance between the code based and the human based grading methodology.
Because in this approach, we are going to be using another large language model to evaluate the output
of the large language model.
You see that here we are actually going to use another LMS to verify the LMS output.
So this is the third evaluation, which to be honest we are going to be using a lot in this entire course.
So all these tools like Deep Evolve or ragas, we are going to be employing the approach of the large
language model as a judge concept or otherwise called as the LMS based evaluation approach to do the
evaluation of the LMS lm's output, because this approach is adequate at handling more intricate and
subjective evaluation compared to the code based grading, while simultaneously offering faster and
greater scalability than the human grading.
And some of these evaluation that you can do using this large language model that judge is these like
GA, valve, Dag, and then Rag metrics like answer, relevancy, faithfulness, contextual precisions,
contextual recall, contextual relevancy, and then the genetic metrics like two comp task completion,
tool completion, and even like conversational metrics, bias, toxicity summarization, prompt hallucination
detections, and things of that nature.
So everything we can do using this LMS as a judge, that's exactly what we'll be talking about in the
Deep Valley in our next section.
But guess what?
This is quite amazing.
That is how we are actually going to do the evaluation.
And these are three different types of evaluation does exist while evaluating a large language model.

=== Set Up Your Testing Environment for LLM Evaluation (DeepEval + RAGAs) ===


--- Every Key Metric You Need to Evaluate AI Apps (Relevancy, Bias & More) ---

In this lecture we're going to talk about understanding evaluation matrices.
So we talked briefly about evaluation matrices in our last lecture of this section where we talked about
some of the following evaluation matrices like answer relevancy faithfulness contextual relevancy,
bias detection context precision, context recall, hallucination detections, tool selection, accuracy,
function, argument accuracy, completeness, or the matrix examples that I have shown over here.
But there are even more matrices available based on the evaluation tools that you select.
There are few tools which has also got hallucination detection, but not every tool has got hallucination
detection.
And we are going to talk about these different tools available in our next lectures.
But you can see that these are some of the most common matrices that you can find with the evaluation
tools.
And let's talk about some of these matrices in a bit more detail.
And most of the matrices you can just guess based on their name.
For example the answer relevance.
So here the evaluation is to assess how relevant is the model's response based on the user query.
And this is same for an application which is built, uh, to use the large language model as well.
So you can use this answer relevancy to check how relevant is the answer that you get back from your
large language model application, or even a large language model which is fine tuned with the relevant
data or a large data set.
So this is how you can check the answer relevance.
So that that is one of the metrics that we most often try to pass in and ensure that we get the relevant
answer for the question that we are asking.
And the next metric is, is the contextual relevancy and contextual precision.
So here the measure is to see how well a response aligns with the retrieved context in the Rag system.
And that is the relevancy of a context.
And if you want to test the contextual precision, it is mainly to assess the precision of the retrieved
context in a Rag system.
So this is two different thing.
I mean the one is the response which aligns with the retrieved context, and the other one is the precision
of the retrieved context in a rack system.
So this is how we measure how the large language model retrieves the data using the rack system which
has been built.
And the next one is the tool selection accuracy.
For instance, if you want to evaluate the chatbots ability to select the appropriate tool or the action
based on the user input, this is where we can ensure that our tools are rightly called while the chatbot
is going to perform any action.
This is very, very important because you are not going to be using 1 or 2 tool built for a large language
model.
While the application keeps growing, we'll be building like hundreds and thousands of tools, which
can be an external tool or an internal tool, or it can be a tool which is more customized to use other
tools among them.
And it will become more and more important that we test the accuracy of the large language model to
choose the right tool for a given input query.
So that is another metrics that we will be using to test how the application and the large language
model selects Select the right tool for the right job, and the next one is the bias detection.
This is very important.
This is mainly used to identify any bias present in the model's response.
And this is where we are going to be testing a large language model to ensure that whether the large
language model split out any bias information.
For example, if you ask a model like Will the boys get more mark than girls?
And if the large language model says yes, then there is a bias there because it is nothing like bias.
Always get higher, Mark, because it depends on the type of questions, and there is no way we can
predict that whether boys or girls get higher.
Mark.
Because if the large language model says yes, then the model is trained with a data set which has got
a data with a lot of boys who are getting higher mark than girls.
And then the model will now start to think that always boys are going to get higher mark than the girls,
but in reality that is not the case.
That is how you can detect the bias within a large language model.
So this is another matrix that we often use to see how the large language model performs and give us
the responses that we're looking for.
And finally, the function argument accuracy.
And this is mainly to assess the correctness of the arguments passed to a function or APIs by a chatbot.
This is also very important to see if you're going to be having multiple functions.
Call within our tools, which are bound in the large language models.
And if we try to test our large language models, tools, calls and we need to see the right arguments
are being passed to the tool call.
If not, the tool call is not going to work, and the tool is just going to respond with no information.
And the large language model in turn will return no answer that you're looking for.
So that is very important to understand whether the right functional arguments are being passed to the
function, so that the function returns the right response for you.
And this is another way of testing whether your large language model application gets the right arguments
from the LM.
So this is another matrices that we use to test.
I mean, there are so many different matrices available as I showed you in the first slide.
So those are the different kinds of testing that we can do to evaluate a large language model and its
applications.


=== DeepEval from the Ground Up: Core Concepts Every AI Tester Must Know ===


--- DeepEval vs RAGAs vs OpenAI Evals vs HuggingFace: Which Tool Wins? ---

In this lecture we're going to talk about evaluation libraries available for us to evaluate the large
language model applications.
So there are many libraries available to evaluate large language models.
And some of the most popular among the communities are deep evals, ragas, OpenAI evals, Galileo Huggingface
evaluate.
So these five of them are most prevalently being used in the industry, and some of them which has got
features which others might not have.
That's the that's the beauty of these tools.
And these tools are still evolving.
They are still trying to bring all the new features that you are, uh, that you're expecting to test
in the large language model applications.
But I'm sure that these applications will now or quickly will have some differentiations to compete,
among others, so that you can choose one among other or maybe all of them.
So we are going to see how these tools are different from others, and which tool is better for evaluating
your large language model application.
So the first one is the OpenAI evolve.
So this eval provides a framework for evaluating large language model applications or systems built
using the Llms.
It offers an existing registry of Eval's to test different dimensions of OpenAI models, and the ability
to write your own custom Eval's for the use cases you care about.
And OpenAI evals is most tightly, uh, intrinsically integrated with the OpenAI models like GPT 4.5
or GPT 4.44.1 and the uh oh models.

application.
It uses the Sota LM assisted methods to quantify the performance of LM application using automated matrices.

index, using it without needing an unaltered evaluation dataset.

--- Unit Tests, Regression Tests & Functional Tests: Applying Old-School QA to LLMs ---

Welcome to the next section of our course.
And in this section we are going to talk about large language model testing itself using deep eval.
But before I start writing the code and make you comfortable with how you can test your large language
model using deep eval tool, first of all, we need to understand the testing of the large language
models in a traditional software testing way.
Because we, software test engineers or software engineers are pretty much confident to do a traditional
software testing with various different techniques.
So I'm just going to relate how we can test our large language model using traditional software testing
way.
Well, LM testing is the process of evaluating an large language model output to ensure it meets all
the specific assessment criteria such as accuracy, coherence, fairness, and safety.
Based on the intended application purposes that we have got.
But the LM testing itself is completely different from the traditional software testing approach, right?
Because evaluating large language models is a complicated process because unlike traditional software
development, where outcomes are predictable and errors can be debugged as a logic can be attributed
to specific code block.
Llms are black box with infinite possibilities of inputs and corresponding outputs.
That is true.
We know that.
But how do we test them all together?
Well, let's take the unit testing approach.
I'm just comparing the traditional test itself.
Really.
So unit testing involves testing the smallest testable part of an application, which for LLM means
evaluating an LLM response for a given input based on some clearly defined criteria, like what we saw
in our earlier lectures of the of this course.
I just said like what is the capital of New Zealand?
Just give me the city name.
The response from the from the model was Wellington, which is great.
I mean this is something that we can immediately test.
This is like a unit test that we can do for our large language model, which is super clear.
But what about functional testing?
Well, functional testing of the large language models involve evaluating large language models on a
specific task as opposed to traditional software functional testing, which is, for example, would
involves verifying whether a user is able to login by testing the entire login flow.
That is what we do in the actual functional testing, right?
But functional testing of the large language model assesses the model's proficiency across a range of
input within a particular task, for example, text summarization.
Or you can also say how you can do an accurate response for a given text that you are given that you
have been given.
So those are things that we can actually do with the functional testing of the large language models.
And similarly regression testing.
So regression testing involves evaluating an LM on same set of test cases every time you make an iteration
to safeguard against breaking changes.
So the upside of using a quantitative LM evaluation metrics for the LM evaluation is we can set clear
threshold to define what is considered a breaking change, and also monitor how the performance of your
LM changes through multiple iterations.
This is quite interesting because the the way if we do this in a better way, we can always see for
any given input the model always respond to the same response that we are looking for.
I mean, at least to the matching context.
Even if the wording changes, if the context is same, then we are pretty good to go.
But if that keeps on changing and hallucinating, then our model is not responding the way we are expecting.
Then the regression test is really failing there.
So those things we can test it as well in the large language models.
And finally responsibility testing.
And this is something not the traditional software development really does, where responsibility testing
is the idea of testing the LM output on responsible AI metrics such as bias, toxicity, fairness,
toxicity and fairness, regardless of the task at hand.
So this is also one thing which is very, very important that we need to test our application.
I mean, I know this is a bit deviation from the traditional testing, but this is also something that
we need to test our large language model to make sure that it works as expected.
So this is how I could compare a large language model in a traditional sense.
But I know there are so many different metrics that we discuss even in our earlier lectures of this
course, like text accuracy and also answer relevance, tool calling, function tool calling, and all
those matrices that we discussed before.
Those things are something which really makes the actual testing of the large language model applications
those things.
As we have already discussed, I'm not going to discuss once again, but just keep all those discussions
that we have did so far theoretically in the mind, so that starting our next lecture, while we start
writing the code, you will have all these ideas in much, much clearer fashion.

--- LLMs-as-a-Judge Explained: Using AI to Grade AI Responses ---

1
In this lecture we are going to talk about using large language model as judge or otherwise
2
called as the LLMS as judge in a short form.
3
So LLMS as a judge is an evaluation methodology where a large language model is employed to
4
assess the output of other large language model based application.
5
Maybe it can be a chatbot, a question answering system or an AI agent or a rack system doesn't matter.
6
You are going to be employing a large language model to assess the output of a large language model.
7
So you can think of this in this way.
8
So you have a large language model which is more capable of running or testing the output
9
of another application which is built using a smaller large language model.
10
Could be maybe QN 2.5 model and you have an application which is going to evaluate the
11
large language model which is going to be of probably a deep seek for that matter.
12
So if you're going to be evaluating the output of a large language model application which
13
is built using a smaller model then you can employ your large language model to do that
14
for those operations.
15
So this is what is the LLM as a judge really.
16
So in this approach the evaluation of the large language model is provided with a specific
17
prompt to rate generated content based on predefined criteria effectively serving as
18
an autonomous evaluator.
19
This is quite cool because now you can see that you don't really have to manually test
20
that the large language model is going to go and evaluate it for you like an automated
21
fashion in here.
22
So why this method is really useful you might know by this time.
23
The large language model as a judge will reduce high costs associated with the human
24
evaluation of the LLM and the LLMS application.
25
By using this technique developers can achieve rapid testing consistent testing and scalable
26
assessment of your large language model application and which is really cool because now you don't
27
necessarily have to worry about how your outputs are going to be.
28
You can just do the testing because sometimes the output could be smaller.
29
But what if the output is super big and you wanted to do the evaluation of it.
30
So in that case a large language model as a judge will be much much better way to do
31
the testing of your large language model applications output.
32
And also large language model as a judge for example in here I'm showing you is the GEvalve
33
metrics which performs much better than the statistically analysis tool that we discussed
34
in our last lecture.
35
If you remember the blue score the rogue and the meteor these scores or the scorers
36
also performs much lesser comparing to the large language models as a judge because this
37
actually does way better than the statistically analysis tool over here as you can see.
38
So this is what is the power of the LLMS as a judge and that is what we are going to be
39
using in most of our evaluation over here.
40
That proves the point that is way better comparatively.
41
But you may ask the question like why are we really talking about this large language
42
model as a judge right now. But guess what.
43
Both the DeepEval and RAGAS are going to utilize this LLMS as a judge concept to evaluate the
44
RAG application or AI application or AI chatbots whatever that you're going to be building.
45
These tools are going to use this LLMS as a judge concept to perform these operations.
46
Now you may be wondering like what does that really mean.
47
Well how is that going to happen.
48
Well guess what.
49
With DeepEval as a LLMS as a judge technique it uses the evaluation techniques and metrics
50
like GAVAL and for RAG it uses the testing of the large language models output using
51

an, uh, hosting provider, pretty much like the language model or the large language model hosting

model as a judge, like all these days that we have been doing so far.

--- What Is RAG & Why Does Your AI App Need It? ---

Welcome to the next section of our course.
And in this section we are going to talk about how we can test a rack based application using deep eval.
Before we talk about how to test a rack based application, let's first understand what is rack based
application.
A rack stands for retrieval Augmented generation, which is an AI system that improves the accuracy
of large language model response by retrieving relevant information from external data sources before
generating the answer.
Because as you know, most of the time, if you try to use a large language model, it will give you
the answer based on the knowledge that it has obtained so far.
For instance, if I go to the local large language model over here, and if I ask for what is MCP.
And if I hit enter right now.
So this is a deep c R1 which is running in my local large language model.
So now it will uh, it will see that it will just think that it is a master control processor or a minimum
credit purchase.
And the thinking is keep on going all the way around.
And now it's giving me the example that MCP stands for several things, depending upon the context.
It says MCP stands for Master controller processor or Minimum credit purchase or methyl cellophane or
whatever.
It's a chemical compound.
It doesn't really go in my tongue.
It's a tongue twister.
But yeah, this is these are the things that it thinks that these are the MCP stands for.
But what I'm looking for is a MCP stands for Model Context Protocol.
But this particular large language model doesn't have any information and context.
And it just gives me this particular answer.
But now let's say if we train this particular model with the model context, protocols, information,
then now the large language model will start thinking about the MCP in that particular angle.
So that is what is this Rag.
The retrieval augmented generation stands for.
So based on the information that you provide to the large language model now it will retrieve the relevant
information from the external data sources before generating the answer itself.
So there are wide range of applications available for this Rag application.
So Rag applications can be used in informations in industries where it requires an accurate up to date
and context aware responses are required.
Some of the examples are like chatbots for instance.
So we just saw this particular example where if we just fuse this with the chatbot, then the answer
may not be relevant.
So we need a relevant information for this particular question.
So chatbots requires this kind of real time accurate and up to date information.
Similarly AI powered search for that matter.
For instance, if you're going to be using an application, for example, your company application or
a company portal where you're looking for a domain specific question which is used within your organization,
and if your application doesn't respond because the model which is trained may not have all these information
that you're looking for.
Then you can fuse the rag paste information into the large language model, so that now your large language
model can get the correct search result based on the query that you are passing in.
And similarly, it's going to be applicable for the financial and business intelligence, healthcare,
e-commerce, and even more.
Really, this list keeps on expanding, but I've just restricted the top five industries and applications.
But you can just think of this like it is going to be keep on expanding.
And it's an ever expanding industries right now because a lot of companies are moving towards AI.
And Rag is the first target application the companies are going with, because the Rag application is
very, very easy to build, and it has a wide range of plugins available to fuse with the large language
model.
So testing a Rag application is critical and very, very important.
The next lecture I will talk about how the Rag application is going to look like in an architectural
perspective.
And we'll also discuss how we can test a rag based application using deep eval.

--- Inside a RAG App: PDF Extraction, Vector Stores & Retrieval Explained ---

In this lecture, we're going to talk about a rack based application and how a rack based application
can get the information from a web or a file, or a PDF file, or a PowerPoint file to get all the information
and store and give the context to the large language model.
Well, this diagram, as you can see over here, is an overview of how a retrieval augmented generation
looks like.
So basically you are going to be extracting the information from the external sources.
And it's going to be split into quite a lot of different chunks.
Or this stage we call it as extracting and indexing really because here we are extracting the information.
We are creating a chunks of file informations because the information needs to be splitted, which can
be stored into a vector database.
And before it stores into a vector database, it needs to be embedded using another model.
So there are a lot of embedding models available.

It can be a olama Obama embedding model, or it can be a GPT embedding model for that matter.
And even there are many different model available with llama as well.

database.

--- AI Agents Explained: Tool Calls, MCP & How They Work Under the Hood ---

Welcome to the next section of our course.
And in this section we are going to talk about testing AI agent tool calling using deep eval.
So far we have discussed how we can test our Rag system using deep Evalf.
With the power of deep eval, we created the multiple data sets for the for the testing of the Rag system.
And then we executed them and we got the response back and things.
So those were all just working fine as expected.
But in this section we are going to see how we can make use of the deep eval to test the AI agent.
But before we talk about testing the AI agent, let's first understand what is this AI agent in first
place?
Well, AI agent is yet another system or very popular system in AI world these days where you can give
the AI or the large language model the ability to talk with the external world, like how we just did
with the Rag system, if you remember.
So over here in the Rag system, the Rag did not have any understanding of the model context protocol.
But once we fuse the large language model with the Rag data, with the vector data stores, and while
it called the vector data store, it gave the response what it has been trained with because now it
has got the knowledge of the external data that it has got, which is the documentation that we asked
it to go and fetch from the internet.
Right.
That's what we did in our last section.
We saw how the deep eval could able to test that, and deep seek could able to retrieve the data from
the external data.
And then it powered the large language model and get the response back to you.
Because before if you ask MCP, it gives some other abbreviation and it gives some other response.
But while we ask the same question, after we gave the Rag data, it gave us the correct response that
we are looking for like model context protocol.
But this could be a limitation with the large language model itself, because you can't always rely
on external data sources that you have got.
But what if we give the ability to the large language model with the access to the internet, or access
to our external systems, or web browsers or file systems, or even your Google Drive or Gmail, something
like that.
If you give these kind of access, just think about it.
Your large language model will become so much more powerful.
And one such example of the AI agent is the Model Context protocol, as you can see over here.
Well, using Model Context Protocol, you can help the AI assistant work more efficiently by connecting
them to a system and tools where the relevant data resides.
And this solves the problem, as you can see over here that we are just talking about the AI models,
no matter how advanced or limited to their ability to access real time or domain specific data due to
the isolated and fragmented systems, and traditionally integrating the AI with the new data source
require custom connectors for each systems, making it hard to scale.
And the one that we just did in our last section, which was the Rag system, that you do a data source
and you do a lot of connections and then make it work.
It is.
It is pretty good.
But the problem is, it is still limited for the question that you ask.
And the I will have the limitation with the data knowledge that it has got.
So MCP or otherwise called as the model context protocol, acts as a universal bridge between the AI
system and the data sources you have got.
And the data sources can be anything really.
It can be a local access of of the data source, which can be your entire git repository, or the access
to the file system or Slack or Google Drive, or even a playwright or puppeteer for that matter.
You can even help to do every single operation using the MCP.
And in a nutshell, MCP makes it easier for the AI system to maintain the context as they interact with
multiple tools and dataset, improving the accuracy and relevancy of their responses.
So this is the power of the Model Context protocol.
And I have also created a MCP playwright with the power of the Model Context protocol over here, as
you can see.
So the playwright model context protocol server tool to automate browsers APIs in the cloud, desktop
client, cursor IDE, and more.
So this is one of the tool that I have built with the power of the MCP.
And this is going to act pretty much like the AI agent, which is going to help your large language
model to talk with the external world.
And one such example I can give you is the cloud desktop, as you can see over here.
So let's say I'm going to ask it like can you navigate to E app.com and perform login operation by clicking
Login link and enter a username and password.
So while I say website you just have to give the accurate website.

particular command now will invoke the playwright local tool.
As you can see over here and this is running the playwright underscore navigate from the playwright

as a decision engine.
And then AI agent is going to act as a router, which is going to route the specific request to a specific
tool which it has bounded with.
And if there is no tool available, then it will just return to the large language model saying, I
don't have any tool that can help you answer this question.
Then the large language model, with its knowledge, it is going to help you respond with the answer
that it knows.
That is what is the AI agent in a nutshell?

--- Zero-Shot Classification & NER: Extracting People, Places & Orgs with LLMs ---

So basically zero shot classification is another way for you to test or label the label the data that
you are passing in, which is very, very handy in real time scenario if you think of is let's say you
have got an email, uh, and you want to mark the email as a spam email or promotional email or maybe

labeling.

This is the reason why this is kind of popular.
And many people do use that, uh, in their in their companies.

Accuracy is mainly used to measure the proportion of correct predictions.
So it's going to say like if the prediction that the large model is doing is actually accurate or not.
So it's based on the name.
You can easily guess what it is.
Right.
And precision it measures the proportion of positive identifications that were actually correct.
So it's like you have got ten positive identifications and five of them are correct and five of them
are not correct.
Then there is like 50 percentage precision.
But if it is like 60% or six percentage, six questions are correct and four of them are wrong, then
it means 60 percentage of precision, something like that.
So you can do all these precisions over here.
And for recall it is a measure of proportion of actual positive that were correctly identified.
It is pretty much like you have got ten correct.
Uh, I mean ten questions in that four is correct.
Then what is the measure of the proportion of actual positives in that in that are correctly identified?
So that is also a recall proportion that you can use.
Similarly there is something called as F1 score which is a harmonic mean of precision and recall.
It's a mean between the precision and recall between both of these over here.
And there's also something called as perplexity which is used for generative task to measure the uncertainty.
This is also very, very helpful.
While we are using a generative models where we are trying to generate an text, and if we want to see
or if you want to measure the uncertainty perplexity score stays one of the most important evaluation
metrics in there.
So you can see that these are the different kinds of evaluations that we can do with the hugging face

--- What Is Component Testing for LLMs? Retrievers, Tools & Traces Explained ---

Welcome to the next section of our course.
And in this section we're going to talk about understanding component level testing of large language
model.
Well if you remember all these days we have been testing the entire application or the entire large
language model application which is evaluating for different matrices.
But now we are going to understand how we can do a component level testing for a fine grained tools.
So basically component level evaluation assesses the individual unit of large language model interaction
between internal components such as retrievers, tool calls, large language model generations, or
even agent to agent interaction with other agents.
Essentially, this will treat the entire large language model as an smaller component instead of treating
it as an entire black box, which we are doing all these days.
So this is the power of the large language models, component level testing.
And this will give you even more handle of what's really going on within each and every component of
our LLM applications.
So just to give you an idea, if you can see this, this is how the interaction within the large language
model happens.
We as a user, we try to interact as a prompt.
It can be an LLM gateway as well.
And then it is going to go invoke a large language model.
And then it is going to either call a tool which can be of a web search or file search, whatever it
is, or if it requires a Rag pipeline to be invoked, then it's going to be invoking as the Rag pipeline.
It's going to perform the operation, and then we are going to be getting the output back.
So this is how things were happening all these days.
But now, because all these application has got so many different components, and we were treating
this entire operation as a black box, we couldn't able to test each and every individuals.
And that's when the component level testing plays a key role.
And it's going to remove the problem that we were facing before.
Well, as I said, what are the different types of application can we test with this component testing.
Well we can test the chatbots.
We can test the rag.
We can test the agents.
We can also do the code generation, testing every single thing.
We can do the testing using this component testing.
But how do we do this component level testing?
Because this is looking pretty new that we have not discussed before.
Well, unlike end to end evaluation, we will need to select a set of appropriate metrics for each components.
We want to evaluate and ensure that the LM test cases class that we had, that we used before, that
we create in that component contains all the necessary parameters.
And that's the only way we can do this component testing better.
And again, there needs a bit of a change or maybe a bit of an additional code to be added within our
component.
And that is something we are going to do using this decorator called as observe, as you can see over
here.
So basically to make an component testable we need to just decorate our existing component with add
observe tag and this will enable your application or enable your component to be testable or otherwise
called as traceable.
So the devil team they call them as tracing.
So essentially you are going to be tracing what's really happening behind the scene for a specific component
while you call a large language model application.
If so far this whole theory is clear to you.
Let's jump into our application that we have been using all these days.
So this is a simple large language model application that we were testing even before.
In our sections, we used to ask a question to the large language model, and this large language model
will go and invoke the necessary toolings and even the Rag application.
And it gets the response to us back.
And for us as a user, we just try to see what is the matrices that it supports.
For example, for the Rag application we go and verify if it has got the correct retrieval context or
the relevancy metrics or contextual metrics, Accuracies, and also the tool selections for the tools,
tool correctness metrics and things.
That's what we were testing all these days using the evaluation that we did before.
But now, because we wanted to not treat this entire box as a black box as you are seeing over here.
But to test them all as an individual component, we are going to introduce these over here.
So we are going to add observe to these components like the large language model and the agent, and
also the tools like this.
And decorating them all with add observe tag.
And now what happens is magically all of these can be tested.
And you know what?
The way in the deep evolve.
You can do the evaluation and you can see the output in the confident, I is going to look something
like this.
As you can see over here, we have this new observatory option with the traces and span where you can
see what exactly is even happening behind the scene for us on what is going on with every single operation.
It can be an agent, it can be a tool, it can be an LLM application.
It can be a custom type of span that we are dealing with.
And now you may think that what is this traces and span.
What exactly are these two things doing over here.
Well this is a terminology, to be honest, that you need to remember.
Well the span, if you just think of is an individual component or an unit of work within your application.
It can be an LLM call.
It can be a tool execution, it can be a retrieval or it can be even large language model application
call.
For that matter, it can be of anything.
Everything is going to be called as span.
So every single individual component of your application is called as span over here.
So just think of these as a span for you over here.
And traces as you can easily imagine is going to be an overall execution flow of your large language
model application.
And it contains multiple Span.
So this is going to contain all the different spans that you're going to be dealing with.
And it's going to be very, very helpful when you trace your application within your deep valve or the
confident eye to see what's going on for a given trace.
So that is exactly what this span and traces are.
And we are going to see a complete demo of how this is all going to work together as well.
As that said, the deep eval team has tried to minimize or reduce the friction of the existing code
as much as possible, and the only thing that you need to add is all about the observe and the component.
And we need to add some update span method, which will make your code to be evaluated as well.
So the way the code is going to look like is going to be something like this.
